such that the related sequence of loops
\{\ell_{s},s\in I\}
(generated according to Description
2.1
) contains a loop
\ell_{s}
which disconnects
i
from
\infty
in
\mathbb{H}
.

In order to prove Proposition
B.3
, we need to first collect a few lemmas.

Lemma B.4
.

For
r>0
, let
D_{r}
be the set of bubbles
e
such that
\sup_{z\in e}|z|=r
. Then
\mu_{\kappa}(D_{r})=0
.

Proof.

Suppose that there exists
r>0
such that
\mu_{\kappa}(D_{r})>0
.
For
s>0
, let
y_{s}(z):=sz
be the scaling of
\mathbb{H}
.
Then
y_{s}(D_{r})=D_{sr}
. By conformal covariance (
3.13
) of
\mu_{\kappa}
, we have

\mu_{\kappa}(D_{sr})=s^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}\mu_{\kappa}(D_{r})>0
. In particular, the
\mu_{\kappa}
measure of the set of loops with
\sup_{z\in e}|z|\in[1,2]
would be infinite, which is impossible. This proves the lemma.
∎

Let
\lambda(z):=(i-z)/(i+z)
be a conformal map from
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathbb{D}
. We use the following metric on
\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle d_{\lambda}(z_{1},z_{2}):=|\lambda(z_{1})-\lambda(z_{2})|\quad\text{ for all }z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{H},

which induces the same topology as the Euclidean metric. Under
d_{\lambda}
,
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
is compact. For any two compact sets
X,Y\subset\overline{\mathbb{H}}
, we also define the Hausdorff distance

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y):=\max\big\{\sup_{x\in X}d_{\lambda}(x,Y),\,\sup_{y\in Y}d_{\lambda}(X,y)\big\}.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
0<s_{1}<\ldots s_{n}
. For all
\delta>0
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
, such that if
t\in(s_{j}-\varepsilon,s_{j}]
for some
1\leq j\leq n
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}}d_{\lambda}\big(f_{t}(z),f_{s_{j}}(z)\big)\leq\delta.

(B.6)

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
B.6
) defines a distance between conformal maps from
\mathbb{H}
onto itself.
The function
s\mapsto f_{s}
is
càglàd
for this distance, because
s\mapsto\Phi_{s^{-}}
is
càglàd
(due to Section
B.1
), and
f_{s}
is given by (
B.4
). By the left-continuity, for each
s_{j}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{j}
, such that (
B.6
) holds for all
t\in(s_{j}-\varepsilon_{j},s_{j}]
. The lemma follows by letting
\varepsilon:=\min(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{j})
.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
h(s):=f_{s}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}
. For
u>0
,
\varepsilon>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
u_{k}:=u+k\varepsilon
. Let

\displaystyle\theta(u,\varepsilon):=\mathbf{1}_{u<s_{0}}\sup_{u\leq s<(u+\varepsilon)\wedge s_{0}}|h(s)-h(u)|,\quad V:=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\theta(u_{k},\varepsilon).

Then
V<\infty
a.s.

Proof.

Taking the derivative of (
B.4
), we get

f_{s}^{\prime}(0)=\Im(\Phi_{s^{-}}(i))/|\Phi_{s^{-}}(i)|^{2}.

Note that for each
s\in[0,s_{0}]
, there exists
t(s)\in[0,\sigma]
such that
\Phi_{s^{-}}=g_{t(s)}
. It follows that
h(s)=H_{t(s)}
where

H_{t}:=\left(\Im(g_{t}(i))/|g_{t}(i)|^{2}\right)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}.

The process
H_{t}
has finite total variation on the finite interval
[0,\sigma]
, because its Ito derivative (using (
B.1
)) has only the
dt
term.
This implies that
h(s)
has finite total variation on the interval
[0,s_{0}]
, which implies the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

There exists a (random)
C>0
, such that for all
0\leq s<t\leq s_{0}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in[0,\pi]}|f_{t}\circ f_{s}^{-1}(re^{i\theta})|\geq Cr.

Proof.

For simplicity, we denote
x_{s}:=\Re\Phi_{s^{-}}(i)
,
y_{s}:=\Im\Phi_{s^{-}}(i)
and
r_{s}:=|\Phi_{s^{-}}(i)|
. Note that
r_{s}^{2}=x_{s}^{2}+y_{s}^{2}
.
By (
B.4
), we have

\displaystyle f_{s}(z)=\frac{-y_{s}z}{x_{s}z-r_{s}^{2}},\quad f_{s}^{-1}(z)=\frac{r_{s}^{2}z}{zx_{s}+y_{s}},\quad f_{t}\circ f_{s}^{-1}(z)=\frac{-y_{t}r_{s}^{2}z}{x_{t}r_{s}^{2}z-x_{s}r_{t}^{2}z-y_{s}r_{t}^{2}}.

Fix
r>0
. We can see that
|f_{t}\circ f_{s}^{-1}(re^{i\theta})|
achieves its supremum when
\theta=0
or
\pi
, and

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in[0,\pi]}|f_{t}\circ f_{s}^{-1}(re^{i\theta})|=\frac{|y_{t}|r_{s}^{2}r}{\left||x_{t}r_{s}^{2}-x_{s}r_{t}^{2}|r-y_{s}r_{t}^{2}\right|}.

(B.7)

As we pointed out in the proof of Lemma
B.6
,
\Phi_{s^{-}}(i)=g_{t(s)}(i)
. The process
g_{t}(i)
is continuous on
[0,\sigma]
, and
g_{t}(i)\not\in\mathbb{R}
for all
t<\sigma
. Moreover,
t(s_{0})<\sigma
a.s., because
t(s_{0})
corresponds to the time that the chordal SLE
{}_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
started to trace the bubble
\ell_{s_{0}}
. This implies that there exists a (random)
\varepsilon>0
, such that

\varepsilon^{-1}\geq\Im g_{t}(i)\geq\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{-1}\geq|g_{t}(i)|\geq\varepsilon
for all
t
in the compact interval
[0,t(s_{0})]
.
This implies that

\varepsilon^{-1}\geq r_{s}\geq y_{s}\geq\varepsilon
for all
s\in[0,s_{0}]
.
For all
r<\varepsilon/2
, (
B.7
) is at least

\displaystyle\frac{|y_{t}|r_{s}^{2}r}{|x_{t}r_{s}^{2}|r+|x_{s}r_{t}^{2}|r+|y_{s}r_{t}^{2}|}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{3}r}{\varepsilon^{-2}+\varepsilon^{-3}}.

The lemma follows by letting
C:=\varepsilon^{6}/(\varepsilon+1)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.3
.

We will construct a sequence of (stopped) Poisson point processes
\mathcal{N}_{n}
which converge to
\mathcal{N}:=\{e_{s},s\in I,s\leq\chi(s_{0})\}
in a certain sense that we will specify.

Step 1. Construction of
\mathcal{N}_{n}
.
Let
\varepsilon:=1/n
. Let
s_{n,0}:=0
. For all
k\geq 0
, if
s_{n,k}<s_{0}
, then let
s_{n,k+1}:=s_{n,k}+f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}\varepsilon
, otherwise let
s_{n,k+1}:=s_{0}
. For all
k\geq 0
, we have
s_{n,k+1}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
. In particular,
s_{n,k}
is a stopping time and
s_{n,k}\not\in J_{0}\setminus\{s_{0}\}
a.s. Conditionally on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
and on
s_{n,k}<s_{0}
, the process

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}:=\{\widetilde{e}_{s_{n,k}+s},s_{n,k}+s\in J_{0},s_{n,k}+s\leq s_{n,k+1}\}

is an independent Poisson point process with parameter
\mu_{\kappa}
, stopped at
(s_{0}-s_{n,k})\wedge(s_{n,k+1}-s_{n,k})
. Note that the Poisson point process itself is independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
, but the stopping time depends on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
. It is clear that
s_{n,k+1}-s_{n,k}
depends on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
, and we explain in the next paragraph the dependence of
s_{0}-s_{n,k}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
.

On the event
s_{n,k}<s_{0}
, let
\Gamma_{k}
be the image under
\Psi_{s_{n,k}}
of the CLE
κ
restricted to
\overline{O_{\chi(s_{n,k})}}
. Conditionally on
s_{n,k}<s_{0}
,
\Gamma_{k}
is distributed as an CLE
κ
and is independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
.
The bubbles in
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
determine a sequence of loops
\{\Psi_{s_{n,k}}(\widetilde{\ell}_{s+s_{n,k}}),s+s_{n,k}\in J_{0},s_{n,k}+s\leq s_{n,k+1}\}
in
\Gamma_{k}
. The time
s_{0}-s_{n,k}
corresponds to the first time that there is a loop in the previous sequence which disconnects
\Psi_{s_{n,k}}(i)
from
\infty
in
\mathbb{H}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
be a new process obtained by rescaling the time of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
by
f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}
. This scaling factor is measurable w.r.t.
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
, but the Poisson point process which defines
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
(without the stopping time) is independent from
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{s_{n,k}}
. Hence the scaling factor acts like a constant. In particular,
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
is still a Poisson point process,
with parameter
f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}\mu_{\kappa}
, stopped at time
(\widetilde{s}_{0}-\widetilde{s}_{n,k})\wedge(\widetilde{s}_{n,k+1}-\widetilde{s}_{n,k})
, where
\widetilde{s}_{0}:=f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}s_{0}
and
\widetilde{s}_{n,j}:=f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}s_{n,j}
for
j=k,k+1
. Note that

\displaystyle\widetilde{s}_{n,k+1}-\widetilde{s}_{n,k}=f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}(s_{n,k+1}-s_{n,k})=\varepsilon.

The time
\tau_{k}:=\widetilde{s}_{0}-\widetilde{s}_{n,k}
is the first time that a loop generated by
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
disconnects
\Psi_{s_{n,k}}(i)
from
\infty
in
\mathbb{H}
(since
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
contains the same ordered sequence of bubbles as
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
, the ordered sequence of loops that it generates is also the same as the one generated by
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
, up to a time-rescaling).

Let
\mathcal{N}_{n,k}
be another new process obtained by taking the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
under
f_{s_{n,k}}
, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{n,k}:=\left\{f_{s_{n,k}}\big(\widetilde{e}_{s_{n,k}+f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}s}\big),\,\,s_{n,k}+f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}s\in J_{0},\,s_{n,k}+f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}s\leq s_{n,k+1}\right\}.

By conformal covariance (
3.13
) of
\mu_{\kappa}
, we deduce that
\mathcal{N}_{n,k}
is still a Poisson point process, with parameter

\displaystyle f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}(f_{s_{n,k}})_{*}\mu_{\kappa}=\mu_{\kappa}.

The stopping time for
\mathcal{N}_{n,k}
is
\varepsilon\wedge\tau_{k}
, the same as
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{n,k}
.

Now, let
\mathcal{N}_{n}
be the concatenation of the processes
\mathcal{N}_{n,0},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{n,k_{0}}
, where
k_{0}
is the biggest
k
such that
s_{n,k}<s_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{n}
is again a Poisson point process, with parameter
\mu_{\kappa}
(same parameter as
\mathcal{N}_{n,k}
) stopped at
T_{n}:=k_{0}\varepsilon+\tau_{k_{0}}
.

Step 2. Convergence of
\mathcal{N}_{n}
to
\mathcal{N}
.
We aim to prove that
\mathcal{N}_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{N}
, in the following sense.

(1)

First, we will show that as
n\to\infty
(recall
\varepsilon=1/n
),

T_{n}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\chi(s_{0}).

(2)

For
\iota>0
, let
A_{\iota}
be the set of bubbles
e
in
\mathbb{H}
rooted at
0
such that
\sup_{z\in e}|z|>\iota
. Suppose that in
\mathcal{N}_{n}
, there are exactly
N_{n}
bubbles in
A_{\iota}
, which occur at times
u_{n,1}<\cdots<u_{n,N_{n}}\leq T_{n}
, denoted by
e^{n}_{u_{n,j}}
for
1\leq j\leq N_{n}
. Suppose that in
\mathcal{N}
, there are exactly
N
bubbles in
A_{\iota}
, which occur at times
u_{1}<\ldots<u_{N}\leq\chi(s_{0})
. We will show that for all
\delta>0
, there exists
n_{0}>0
, such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have
N_{n}=N
and
|u_{n,j}-u_{j}|\leq\delta
,
d_{\mathrm{H}}(e^{n}_{u_{n,j}},e_{u_{j}})\leq\delta
for all
1\leq j\leq N
.

Let us first show (
1
). By definition, we have

|s_{0}-s_{n,k_{0}}|\leq f_{s_{n,k_{0}}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}\varepsilon.

By Lemma
B.6
, we have
\sup_{s\in[0,s_{0}]}f_{s}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}<V<\infty
. So
|s_{0}-s_{n,k_{0}}|\to 0
. Since
\chi
is continuous, we also have
|\chi(s_{0})-\chi(s_{n,k_{0}})|\to 0
.
On the other hand, by (
B.5
), for each
1\leq k\leq k_{0}-1
,

\displaystyle|\chi(s_{n,k})-k\varepsilon|=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\bigg|\int_{s_{n,j}}^{s_{n,j+1}}f_{u}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}du-\varepsilon\bigg|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\theta(s_{n,j},\varepsilon)\varepsilon\leq V\varepsilon\to 0,

(B.8)

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
B.6
. Combining with
|\chi(s_{0})-\chi(s_{n,k_{0}})|\to 0
, we get
|\chi(s_{0})-k_{0}\varepsilon|\to 0
. Note that
\tau_{k_{0}}\leq\varepsilon
by definition, hence (
1
) follows.

Let us now show (
2
). If we define
N(u)
to be the number of bubbles in
A_{\iota}
in
\mathcal{N}
up to time
u
, then
N(u)
is
càdlàg
and has jumps of size
1
. In particular,
N=N(\chi(s_{0}))<\infty
a.s.
For each
1\leq j\leq N
, let
\widetilde{u}_{j}:=\chi^{-1}(u_{j})
. We have
f_{\widetilde{u}_{j}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}_{j}})=e_{u_{j}}\in A_{\iota}
.
Let
z_{j}\in e_{u_{j}}
be the point that achieves
\sup_{z\in e_{u_{j}}}|z|
. Let

\displaystyle\delta_{0}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq N}\inf\{d_{\lambda}(z_{j},z):z\in\partial\mathcal{B}(0,\iota)\cap\mathbb{H}\}.

Note that
\delta_{0}>0
, because
A_{\iota}
is an open set w.r.t.
d_{\mathrm{H}}
, and
N<\infty
.
Since
\widetilde{u}_{j}\in[0,s_{0}]
and
s_{n,k_{0}}\to s_{0}
as
n\to\infty
, for
n
big enough, say
n\geq n_{0}
, there is a unique integer
k(j)\in[0,k_{0}-1]
such that
s_{n,k(j)}\leq\widetilde{u}_{j}<s_{n,k(j)+1}
. By Lemma
B.5
, for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}/2)
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}
, such that if
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, then

d_{\mathrm{H}}\big(f_{s_{n,k(j)}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}_{j}}),f_{\widetilde{u}_{j}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}_{j}})\big)\leq\delta\,\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq N.

It follows that
f_{s_{n,k(j)}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}_{j}})\in A_{\iota}
for
1\leq j\leq N
, hence these bubbles contribute to
N_{n}
. We increase
n_{0}
so that
\varepsilon_{n_{0}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, then
N_{n}\geq N
for
n\geq n_{0}
. By Step 1, for each
1\leq j\leq N
,
\widetilde{u}_{j}
corresponds to the time

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{j}=k(j)\varepsilon+f_{s_{n,k(j)}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}(\widetilde{u}_{j}-s_{n,k(j)})

in the process
\mathcal{N}_{n}
. Since
u_{j}=\chi(\widetilde{u}_{j})
, similarly to (
B.8
), we have

\displaystyle|u_{j}-\widehat{u}_{j}|\leq|\chi(s_{n,k(j)})-k(j)\varepsilon|+\left|\int_{s_{n,k(j)}}^{\widetilde{u}_{j}}f_{u}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}du-f_{s_{n,k}}^{\prime}(0)^{(8-\kappa)/\kappa}(\widetilde{u}_{j}-s_{n,k(j)})\right|\leq V\varepsilon.

By increasing
n_{0}
, we can let
|u_{j}-\widehat{u}_{j}|\leq\delta
.

To complete the proof of (
2
), it remains to show that
N_{n}\leq N
for
n
big enough.
For the sake of contradiction, suppose that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, there is a time
\widehat{u}^{n}
in
[0,T_{n}]\setminus\{\widehat{u}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{u}_{N}\}
such that the bubble in
\mathcal{N}_{n}
at
\widehat{u}^{n}
is in
A_{\iota}
. Let
k_{n}
be the unique integer in
[0,k_{0}]
such that
k_{n}\varepsilon\leq\widehat{u}^{n}<(k_{n}+1)\varepsilon
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{n}:=s_{n,k_{n}}+f_{s_{n,k_{n}}}^{\prime}(0)^{(\kappa-8)/\kappa}(\widehat{u}^{n}-k_{n}\varepsilon).

(B.9)

Then
f_{s_{n,k_{n}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}^{n}})\in A_{\iota}
, and
u^{n}:=\chi(\widetilde{u}^{n})\in[0,\chi(s_{0})]\setminus\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}\}
.
This means that
e_{u^{n}}\not\in A_{\iota}
, namely
\sup_{z\in e_{u^{n}}}|z|\leq\iota
.
We have

\displaystyle e_{u^{n}}=f_{\widetilde{u}^{n}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{e}_{n}})=f_{\widetilde{u}^{n}}\circ f_{s_{n,k_{n}}}^{-1}\left(f_{s_{n,k_{n}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}^{n}})\right).

Since
f_{s_{n,k_{n}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}^{n}})\in A_{\iota}
, by Lemma
B.7
, we have
e_{u^{n}}\in A_{C\iota}
. Suppose that
n(j)
is a subsequence such that
\widetilde{u}^{n(j)}\to\widetilde{u}
as
j\to\infty
, then
u^{n(j)}\to u=\chi(\widetilde{u})
.
The subsequence
(e_{u^{n(j)}})_{j\geq 0}
must be stablized after some
j_{0}
, equal to
e_{u}
for some
u\in I
, because otherwise, there would be infinitely many bubbles in
\mathcal{N}
in
A_{C\iota}
, leading to a contradiction. Note that
e_{u}\in A_{C\iota}\setminus A_{\iota}
. By (
B.9
), we also have
s_{n(j),k_{n(j)}}\to\widetilde{u}
. By Lemma
B.5
, for all
\delta_{1}>0
, there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, such that if
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
, then for all
j\geq j_{0}
(where we have possibly increased
j_{0}
so that
n(j_{0})\geq 1/\varepsilon_{1}
),

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\big(f_{s_{n(j),k_{n(j)}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}}),e_{u}\big)=d_{\mathrm{H}}\big(f_{s_{n(j),k_{n(j)}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}}),f_{\widetilde{u}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}})\big)\leq\delta_{1}.

Since
f_{s_{n(j),k_{n(j)}}}(\widetilde{e}_{\widetilde{u}})\in A_{\iota}
, and
e_{u}\not\in A_{\iota}
, the only possibility is that
\sup_{z\in e_{u}}|z|=\iota
. However, this is impossible due to Lemma
B.4
.
This proves that
N_{n}\leq N
for
n\geq n_{0}
, and completes the proof of (
2
).

Step 3. Conclusion.
In Step 1, we have constructed
\mathcal{N}_{n}
, which is a Poisson point process with parameter
\mu_{\kappa}
, stopped at
T_{n}
. The result of Step 2 implies that
\mathcal{N}_{n}
converges a.s. to
\mathcal{N}
as point processes. By definition,
\mathcal{N}
is stopped at
\chi(s_{0})
, and
\chi(s_{0})
is a stopping time for
\mathcal{N}
. To see the Poisson point process nature of
\mathcal{N}
, one can first construct the unstopped versions of
\mathcal{N}_{n}
and
\mathcal{N}
, by concatenating to each of them (after their respective stopping time) a same independent Poisson point process with parameter
\mu_{\kappa}
. Then the unstopped versions of
\mathcal{N}_{n}
(which are Poisson point processes) converge a.s. to the unstopped version of
\mathcal{N}
, which is consequently also Poisson point processes with parameter
\mu_{\kappa}
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
B.3
.
∎

Now, we are ready to conclude the proof of Description
2.1
.

Proof of Description
2.1
.

Proposition
B.3
shows that
\{e_{s},s\in I,s\leq\chi(s_{0})\}
is a Poisson point process stopped at the first time that a loop in
\{\ell_{s},s\in I\}
disconnects
i
from
\infty
in
\mathbb{H}
. If
\overline{\overline{e_{\chi(s_{0})}}}
encircles
i
, then the stopping time
\tau
in Description
2.1
is equal to
\chi(s_{0})
. Otherwise, let
D_{i}
be the connected component containing
i
of
\mathbb{H}\setminus e_{\chi(s_{0})}
. Let
f
be the conformal map from
D_{i}
onto
\mathbb{H}
with
f(i)=i
and
f^{\prime}(i)>0
.
Conditionally on
\{e_{s},s\in I,s\leq\chi(s_{0})\}
, the image under
f
of the CLE
κ
restricted to
\overline{D}_{i}
is equal to an independent CLE
κ
in
\mathbb{H}
. We can then iterate the same exploration process for this new CLE
κ
. The concatenation of these iterated processes construct the Poisson point process in Description
2.1
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Proposition
3.15

We first recall the construction of the
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
bubble measure via a limiting procedure due to
[
Zha22
]
. The
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
bubble measure is defined by
\mathsf{m}_{\gamma^{2}}:=c(\gamma)\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varepsilon^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-1}\mathsf{m}^{\varepsilon}_{\gamma^{2}}
, where
\mathsf{m}^{\varepsilon}_{\gamma^{2}}
is the law of a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
from
0
to
-\varepsilon
in
\mathbb{H}
with the force point being
0^{+}
. We choose the constant
c(\gamma)
such that
\mathsf{m}_{\gamma^{2}}[E_{i}]=1
, where
E_{i}
is the event that
i
is enclosed by the loop sampled from
\mathsf{m}_{\gamma^{2}}
. Let
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
be the restriction of
\mathsf{m}_{\gamma^{2}}
on the event
E_{i}
.
The conformal welding result for the
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
bubble measure can be stated as follows.

Proposition C.1
.

Proposition
3.15
holds with
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
replaced by
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
.

Proposition
C.1
is a special case of
Wu’s result
[
Wu23
, Theorem 1.1]
on the conformal welding for the
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\rho)
bubble measure. The argument for this case works for all
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\rho)
with
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
\rho\in(-2,\gamma^{2}/2-2)
, which we sketch it in Section
C.1
for completeness. In Section
C.2
, we prove that
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}=\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
which will conclude the proof of Proposition
3.15
.

C.1
Sketch of the proof of Proposition
C.1

The proof consists of two steps.

Step 1.
This step is a straightforward adaptation of an argument from
[
ARS21
, Section 4]
with
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}/2)
there replaced by
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)
. We start with the conformal welding for chordal
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
from
[
AHS20
]
. For
W=\gamma^{2}-2
and some
C\in(0,\infty)
we have

\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(W+2)\times{\rm chordal}\mbox{ }{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(W-2)=C{\rm Weld}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(W),\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(2)).

(C.1)

We first add a marked point to the sample from
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(2)
and embed the resulting welded surface to
\mathbb{H}
with the starting point of the interface at 0 and the bulk marked point at
i
. Then the endpoint
y
of the interface becomes a random point lying on
\mathbb{R}
. Conditioning on
y
, the law of the interface is the chordal
\mathrm{SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)

from
0
to
y
conditioned on the event that
i
is on its left. Now fix
\varepsilon
small, we restrict to the event that the quantum length of the interval
(y,0)
is between
\varepsilon
and
2\varepsilon
. At the quantum surface level, this conditioning will not affect the sample from
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)
, but change the other surface to a sample from
\rm QD_{1,1}
. Hence the resulting picture is
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\rm QD_{1,1})
. On the other hand, the point
y
converges to
0
hence the law of the interface will converge to
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
. Moreover, this convergence holds even after we freeze the randomness of the field. This way, we get that the conformal welding of a sample from
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\rm QD_{1,1})
can be described by
(\mathbb{H},i,0,\phi,\eta)
, where
(\phi,\eta)
is sampled from the product measure
\mathbb{F}\times\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
, for some law
\mathbb{F}
on fields.

Step 2.
To conclude the proof, we must show that
\mathbb{F}=C_{3}{\rm LF}_{\mathbb{H}}^{(\gamma,i),(\frac{4}{\gamma}-\gamma,0)}
for some constant
C_{3}\in(0,\infty)
. We first consider the conformal welding
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk,\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\rm QD_{0,1})
.
Let
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3})
be a sample from
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)\times\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(2)\times\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)
. Then by de-weighting the quantum area of the middle surface in Lemma
3.5
, the concatenation of
(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3})
has the law of
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)
with a marked bead from the counting measure on all of its beads. Let us denote its law by
\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk,\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2)
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.18
,
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk,\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\rm QD_{0,1})
equals
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(2),\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\widetilde{\rm QD}_{0,3})
. By repeatedly applying the conformal welding result for quantum triangles from
[
ASY22
]
(see the proof of Lemma
3.19
), we obtain a quantum triangle with three weights given by
2\gamma^{2}-2,\gamma^{2}+2,\gamma^{2}+2
. The three corresponding log singularities are
\beta_{0}=\frac{4}{\gamma}-\gamma,0,0
, respectively.
Once we embed the welded surface to
(\mathbb{H},-1,0,1)
with
0
being the
\beta_{0}
-insertion, we get a sample from
\mathrm{LF}^{(\beta_{0},0)}_{\mathbb{H}}\times m
, where
m
is a measure on simple curves that pass
-1,0,1
.
Now we consider the conformal welding
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk,\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),\rm QD_{1,1})
. Once it is embedded to
(\mathbb{H},-1,0,1)
, the law of the field
\phi
, the curve
\eta
and the additional bulk marked point
z
is given by
1_{E_{z,\eta}}e^{\gamma\phi(z)}dz\cdot\mathrm{LF}^{(\beta_{0},0)}_{\mathbb{H}}(d\phi)\times m(d\eta)
, where
E_{z,\eta}
is the event that
z
is in the region bounded by
\eta
that corresponds to
\rm QD_{1,1}
. By the coordinate change formula of Liouville field (Lemma
3.9
), if we embed a sample of
\rm Weld(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk,\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2),{\rm QD}_{1,1})
to
(\mathbb{H},i,0)
where
i
is the bulk insertion and
0
has the
\beta_{0}
-log singularity, then we get
\mathrm{LF}^{(\gamma,i),(\beta_{0},0)}\times m^{\bullet,\bullet}
, where
m^{\bullet,\bullet}
is a measure on simple curves on
\mathbb{H}
with two additional marked points on
\partial\mathbb{H}
.

Comparing the conclusions from Step 1 and Step 2, we see that modulo a multiplicative constant the measure
m^{\bullet,\bullet}
can be obtained from
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
as follows. First sample
\eta
from
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
, then choose a connected component
B
of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta
according to the counting measure, and finally choose the two endpoints of
\partial B\cap\mathbb{H}
as the two additional marked points. On the other hand, the field measure
\mathbb{F}
must equal
C_{3}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbb{H}}^{(\gamma,i),(\beta_{0},0)}
for some
C_{3}>0
. The finiteness of
C_{3}
can be seen from the fact that the event that the total boundary length lies in
[1,2]
is finite on both sides. This concludes the proof of Proposition
C.1
.

Remark C.1
.

Recall the setting of Lemma
2.7
, which asserts
\mathbb{E}[|\psi_{e_{\tau}}^{\prime}(i)|^{\zeta}]<\infty
for
\zeta<1-2/\kappa
.
We now recall the exact formula for
\mathbb{E}[|\psi_{e_{\tau}}^{\prime}(i)|^{\zeta}]
from
[
Wu23
, Proposition 7.12]
and
[
ARSZ23
, Subsection 4.3]
which is based on the conformal welding result in Proposition
C.1
. Let
\beta=\frac{4}{\gamma}-\gamma\in(0,\gamma)
. In our notation, the second equation above Lemma 4.3 in
[
ARSZ23
]
states that for any
Q-\frac{\beta}{2}<\alpha<Q
,

\mathbb{E}[|\psi_{e_{\tau}}^{\prime}(i)|^{2-2\Delta_{\alpha}}]=\frac{c_{\gamma,\beta}\Gamma(\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha)}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2}+\frac{\gamma\beta}{4}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha\pm\frac{\beta}{2})}\frac{(\alpha-Q)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}-Q)}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)+1)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(\frac{\beta}{2}+\alpha-Q))\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(2Q-\alpha-\frac{\beta}{2})}.

Here
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(x)
is the double Gamma function and
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha\pm\frac{\beta}{2})
means
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha-\frac{\beta}{2})
. It is straightforward to verify that the right-hand side is analytic for
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
(when
\alpha=Q-\frac{\beta}{2}
, both
\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(\frac{\beta}{2}+\alpha-Q))
and
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\alpha+\frac{\beta}{2}-Q)
have a single pole so the right-hand side is still analytic). Therefore, the analytic continuation argument in Lemma
4.15
yields that
\mathbb{E}[|\psi_{e_{\tau}}^{\prime}(i)|^{\zeta}]<\infty
for all
\zeta<2-2\Delta_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}=1-\frac{2}{\kappa}
. This proves Lemma
2.7
.

C.2
Duality for the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
bubble measure

We now prove
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}=\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
as stated in Lemma
C.3
, which is an instance of the
{\rm SLE}
duality.
The proof follows from the limiting construction of
{\rm SLE}
bubble measures and the classical duality for chordal SLE. Recall from (
2.15
) that
\mu_{\kappa}=c(\kappa)\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varepsilon^{-1+8/\kappa}\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
where
\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
denotes the probability law of a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve from
0
to
-\varepsilon
. The constant
c(\kappa)
is chosen such that
\mu_{\kappa}[E_{i}]=1
where
E_{i}
denotes the event that
i
is enclosed by the outer boundary of the loop. The first step is to show that their outer boundaries also converge in law, as shown in Lemma
C.2
.

Lemma C.2
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, let
\widehat{\mu}_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
be the conditioning law of
\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
on the event that the outer boundary of a loop encloses
i
. Then,
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\overline{\widehat{\mu}_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}}=\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

Fix small
r>0
. Let
\mu_{\kappa,r}^{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\mu_{\kappa,r}
) be the probability measure obtained by conditioning
\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\mu_{\kappa}
) on the event that the curve intersects
\partial B_{r}(0)
.
By (
2.15
), we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\mu_{\kappa,r}^{\varepsilon}=\mu_{\kappa,r}
. Let
\eta

and
\eta^{\varepsilon}
be curves sampled from
\mu_{\kappa,r}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mu_{\kappa,r}
, respectively. For concreteness we parameterize
\eta
and
\eta^{\varepsilon}
by
[0,1]
. By Skorohod’s representation theorem, we can find a coupling such that
\eta^{\varepsilon}
converges to
\eta
almost surely as
\varepsilon
tends to zero in the uniform topology.

Let
\tau^{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\tau
) be the first time that
\eta^{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\eta
) hits
\partial B_{r}(0)
.
Let
g^{\varepsilon}
(resp.
g
) be the conformal map from the infinite connected component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\varepsilon}[0,\tau^{\varepsilon}]
(resp.
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta[0,\tau]
) to
\mathbb{H}
such that
(\eta^{\varepsilon}(\tau^{\varepsilon}),\infty,-\varepsilon)
(resp.
(\eta(\tau),\infty,0)
) is mapped to
(0,\infty,-1)
. Then
g^{\varepsilon}
converges to
g
uniformly on any compact set away from
B_{r}(0)
.
Let
\eta_{1}^{\varepsilon}=g^{\varepsilon}(\eta^{\varepsilon}[\tau^{\varepsilon},1])
and
\eta_{1}=g(\eta[\tau,1])
. By domain Markov property, given
\eta^{\varepsilon}[0,\tau^{\varepsilon}]
and
\eta[0,\tau]
, both
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\eta_{1}
has the same law as a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
-1
. Therefore, we can re-sample
\eta^{\varepsilon}[\tau^{\varepsilon},1]
and
\eta[\tau,1]
so that in our coupling
\eta_{1}^{\varepsilon}=\eta_{1}
.

Let
\overline{\overline{\eta}}
(resp.
\overline{\overline{\eta}}^{\varepsilon}
) be the outer boundary of
\eta
(resp.
\eta^{\varepsilon}
). Under our coupling, we must have
\overline{\overline{\eta}}^{\varepsilon}
converge to

\overline{\overline{\eta}}
within any compact set away from
B_{r}(0)
. This remains true if we further condition on the event that
i
is enclosed by
\overline{\overline{\eta}}
or
\overline{\overline{\eta}}^{\varepsilon}
.
Now sending
r
to 0 we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\overline{\widehat{\mu}_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}}=\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
. ∎

Lemma C.3
.

We have
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}=\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
.

Proof.

By
{\rm SLE}
duality (see
[
Zha08
]
), we see that the outer boundary of
\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}
has the same law as a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}-2,\gamma^{2}-4)
from
0
to
-\varepsilon
with the force points being
0^{-}
and
0^{+}
. We will denote its law by
\mathsf{n}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathsf{n}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
be the conditioning law of
\mathsf{n}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
to the event that
i
is enclosed by the curve. Then
\overline{\mu_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}}=\mathsf{n}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
and thus
\overline{\widehat{\mu}_{\kappa}^{\varepsilon}}=\widehat{\mathsf{n}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
. Combined with Lemma
C.2
, we see that
\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\widehat{\mathsf{n}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}=\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
.

Recall that
\mathsf{m}^{\varepsilon}_{\gamma^{2}}
is the law of a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
from
0
to
-\varepsilon
with the force point being
0^{+}
. Although
\mathsf{n}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}\neq\mathsf{m}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
due to the additional force point at
0^{-}
, we can coupling them together and prove
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}=\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
by sending
\varepsilon\to 0
. Fix small
\varepsilon>0
.
Let
(\eta^{\varepsilon},\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0})
be a pair of
\mathrm{SLE}
curves where the marginal law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}
is chordal

{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(\gamma^{2}-4)
on
\mathbb{H}
from
0
to
-\varepsilon
with the force point being
0^{+}
, and conditioning on
\eta^{\varepsilon}
, the law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0}
is a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\gamma^{2}}(-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2},\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}-2)
from
0
to
-\varepsilon
in the domain bounded by
\eta^{\varepsilon}
and
[-\varepsilon,0]
. The existence of such a pair is ensured by imaginary geometry
[
MS16
]
. Now we condition on the event that
i
is enclosed by
\eta^{\varepsilon}
, and we denote this conditioned probability measure by
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}
. The marginal law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}
under
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}
is
\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, under
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}
the diameter of
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0}
converges to 0 in probability.

Let
G^{\varepsilon}
be the event that
i
is not enclosed by
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0}
. Then
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}[G^{\varepsilon}]=1-o_{\varepsilon}(1)
.
On this event, let
f^{\varepsilon}
be the conformal map from the unbounded connected component of
\mathbb{H}\setminus\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0}
to
\mathbb{H}
such that
f^{\varepsilon}(0)=0
and
f^{\varepsilon}(i)=i
. Let
x_{\varepsilon}=f^{\varepsilon}(-\varepsilon)<0
and
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2}=f^{\varepsilon}(\eta^{\varepsilon})
. Then the conditional law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2}
is
\widehat{\mathsf{n}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{x_{\varepsilon}}
. Sending
\varepsilon\to 0
, this conditional law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{2}
weakly converges to
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
. On the other hand, since
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}[G^{\varepsilon}]=1-o_{\varepsilon}(1)
and the diameter of
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{0}
converges to 0, the law of
\eta^{\varepsilon}
under
\mathbb{P}^{\varepsilon}
also converges to the same limit
\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
. Therefore
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}=\overline{\widehat{\mu}}_{\kappa}
. Since
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}^{\varepsilon}=\widehat{\mathsf{m}}_{\gamma^{2}}
, we conclude.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.15
.

Combine Proposition
C.1
and Lemma
C.3
.
∎
